## GHSA-g4c3-4g96-6g4m · chamilo/chamilo-lms

2026-09-17 · PHP · **CRITICAL** 9.8 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTH→ENDPOINT`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from a combination of factors: lack of authentication on the file upload endpoint, insufficient sanitization of filenames, and the ability to control the final filename during the 'finish' action. An unauthenticated attacker could upload arbitrary files, including PHP scripts, and then manipulate the filename to execute them on the server.

### Impact

An unauthenticated attacker could achieve remote code execution on the Chamilo LMS server, leading to full compromise of the application and potentially the underlying system.

### Key diff

```
--- a/public/plugin/CStudio/editor/import-project/inc/big-upload.php
+++ b/public/plugin/CStudio/editor/import-project/inc/big-upload.php
@@ -240,7 +240,7 @@ public function finishUpload($finalName, $scormid)
      */
     public function postUnsupported($scormid)
     {
-        $name = $_FILES['bigUploadFile']['name'];
+        $name = disable_dangerous_file(api_replace_dangerous_char((string) ($_FILES['bigUploadFile']['name'] ?? '')));
         $tempName = $_FILES['bigUploadFile']['tmp_name'];
 
         if (filesize($tempName) > self::MAX_SIZE) {
@@ -257,6 +257,13 @@ public function postUnsupported($scormid)
     }
 }
 
+// Require an authenticated Chamilo session
+if (empty(api_get_user_id())) {
+    http_response_code(403);
+    echo json_encode(['errorStatus' => 1, 'errorText' => 'Forbidden']);
+    exit;
+}
+
 // Instantiate the class
 $bigUpload = new BigUpload();
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces an authentication check at the beginning of the `big-upload.php` script, ensuring only authenticated users can access the functionality. It also implements `disable_dangerous_file` and `api_replace_dangerous_char` functions for filename sanitization. Crucially, the final filename is now registered in the user's session with proper sanitization when the first chunk is uploaded, preventing an attacker from controlling it during the 'finish' action.

## GHSA-6j36-r6pr-59x4 · @vendure/core

2026-09-17 · JavaScript · **CRITICAL** 9.1 · Pattern: `MISSING_VERIFICATION→SIGNATURE`

### Root cause

The system allowed external authentication methods to link to existing user accounts based solely on an email address, without verifying that the external provider had confirmed ownership of that email. An attacker could register with an external provider using a victim's email (if the provider doesn't verify email ownership) and then use this to link to and take over the victim's existing account in Vendure.

### Impact

An attacker could take over any existing user account by providing an unverified email address matching a victim's account through an external authentication strategy, gaining full access to the victim's data and functionality.

### Key diff

```
-        if (existingUser) {
+        if (existingUser) {
+            if (!config.verified) {
+                throw new UnverifiedExternalEmailError();
+            }
             user = existingUser;
         } else {
             const customerRole = await this.roleService.getCustomerRole(ctx);
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a check during external authentication. If an external identity attempts to link to an existing account, it now verifies that the external provider has marked the email as 'verified'. If the email is unverified, the linking attempt is rejected with an 'UnverifiedExternalEmailError', preventing account takeover.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 54 occurrences**

## GHSA-rfx3-98h7-v3xp · Marten

2026-09-17 · C# · **CRITICAL** 9.1 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→SQL`

### Root cause

The Marten LINQ provider and internal operations were constructing SQL queries by directly concatenating user-controlled input (like tenant IDs, dictionary keys, and projected constant strings) into SQL string literals without proper escaping or parameterization. Although some inputs were expected to be validated, the system did not consistently enforce this, leading to scenarios where unvalidated input could be directly embedded.

### Impact

An attacker could inject arbitrary SQL commands into the database, potentially leading to data exfiltration, modification, or deletion, and could bypass tenant isolation to access or manipulate data belonging to other tenants.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/Marten/Internal/Operations/DeleteAllForTenant.cs
+++ b/src/Marten/Internal/Operations/DeleteAllForTenant.cs
@@ -40,9 +42,8 @@ public void ConfigureCommand(ICommandBuilder builder, IStorageSession session)
     public void ConfigureCommand(ICommandBuilder builder, IStorageSession session)
     {
         var name = _tableName ?? session.StorageFor(DocumentType).TableName.QualifiedName;
-        builder.Append($"delete from {name} where tenant_id = '");
-        builder.Append(_tenantId);
-        builder.Append("'");
+        builder.Append($"delete from {name} where tenant_id = ");
+        builder.AppendParameter(_tenantId);
     }
 
     public Type DocumentType { get; }
```

### Fix summary

The patch addresses the SQL injection vulnerabilities by ensuring that all user-controlled inputs used in SQL queries are either properly parameterized or, when used as string literals (e.g., JSON path keys or tenant IDs for partition pruning), are explicitly escaped to prevent SQL injection. Specifically, single quotes in these inputs are now replaced with double single quotes ('').

## GHSA-hp3v-5vw7-fx9w · RestrictedPython

2026-09-17 · Python · **HIGH** 8.4 · Pattern: `DESERIALIZATION→OBJECT_INJECTION`

### Root cause

The `RestrictedPython` library failed to properly restrict access to `string.Formatter` and its methods. An attacker could instantiate `string.Formatter` and use its field resolution logic (`get_field`, `get_value`, `format`, `vformat`) to bypass the sandbox and access arbitrary objects and attributes, including those intended to be restricted.

### Impact

An attacker could execute arbitrary code or access sensitive data outside the intended restricted execution environment, leading to full system compromise or data exfiltration.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/RestrictedPython/Guards.py
+++ b/src/RestrictedPython/Guards.py
@@ -254,6 +257,14 @@ def safer_getattr(object, name, default=None, getattr=getattr):
             (isinstance(object, type) and issubclass(object, str))):
         raise NotImplementedError(
             'Using the format*() methods of `str` is not safe')
+    if object is string and name == 'Formatter':
+        raise NotImplementedError('string.Formatter is not safe')
+    if name in _FORMATTER_UNSAFE_METHODS and (
+            isinstance(object, string.Formatter) or
+            (isinstance(object, type) and
+             issubclass(object, string.Formatter))):
+        raise NotImplementedError(
+            'Using string.Formatter methods is not safe')
```

### Fix summary

The patch explicitly disallows access to `string.Formatter` itself and prevents the use of specific methods (`format`, `get_field`, `get_value`, `vformat`) on `string.Formatter` instances or subclasses within the restricted environment. This closes the vector for sandbox escape through string formatting mechanisms.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 6 occurrences**
> Languages: Java, PHP, JavaScript, Python
> → GHSA-rmj7-2vxq-3g9f
> → GHSA-j3rv-43j4-c7qm
> → GHSA-9369-69wj-7m2f
> → GHSA-337j-9hxr-rhxg
> → GHSA-w23p-wrp7-ch38

🔗 [View commit](https://github.com/zopefoundation/RestrictedPython/commit/3b47440070b91f8807c2b2998aca99e94783639a)

## GHSA-7grg-jcf7-rpmx · org.asynchttpclient:async-http-client

2026-09-17 · Java · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The AsyncHttpClient library, when processing compressed HTTP responses, did not impose a limit on the size to which a compressed body could decompress. This unbounded decompression allowed a malicious server to send a small, highly compressed response that would expand to an extremely large size, consuming excessive client memory.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a 'decompression bomb' to send to clients using AsyncHttpClient. When the client attempts to decompress this response, it would exhaust its memory resources, leading to a denial of service for the client application.

### Key diff

```
--- a/client/src/main/java/org/asynchttpclient/AsyncHttpClientConfig.java
+++ b/client/src/main/java/org/asynchttpclient/AsyncHttpClientConfig.java
@@ -359,14 +358,19 @@ default boolean isStripAuthorizationOnRedirect() {
   }
 
   /**
-   * The maximum number of bytes a compressed response body may inflate to. Guards against a
-   * "decompression bomb": a small response that expands without bound once decoded. {@code 0} disables the
-   * limit and restores Netty's unbounded behaviour.
+   * The maximum number of bytes a compressed response body may inflate to, counted over the whole
+   * response. Guards against a "decompression bomb": a small response that expands without bound once
+   * decoded. {@code 0} disables the limit.
+   * <p>
+   * The literal is hardcoded rather than read through {@link AsyncHttpClientConfigDefaults}: this default
+   * runs for any third-party implementation of this interface, and it is reached on the event loop when
+   * the pipeline is built, so a property lookup plus {@code Integer.parseInt} would turn a typo in a
+   * configuration file into a failure on every connection rather than at startup.
    *
    * @return the decompressed response ceiling in bytes
    */
   default int getMaxDecompressedResponseSize() {
-    return AsyncHttpClientConfigDefaults.defaultMaxDecompressedResponseSize();
+    return 268435456;
   }
 
   enum ResponseBodyPartFactory {
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new configuration option, `maxDecompressedResponseSize`, which defaults to 256 MB. A new `BoundedHttpContentDecompressor` handler is added to the Netty pipeline. This handler monitors the cumulative size of the decompressed response and throws a `DecompressionException` if the configured limit is exceeded, preventing memory exhaustion.

## GHSA-mggc-4xg6-vcxf · SSH.NET

2026-09-17 · C# · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→COMMAND`

### Root cause

The ScpClient in SSH.NET by default used `RemotePathTransformation.DoubleQuote` for remote paths. While this provides some protection, it is insufficient for all server types, particularly shell-based servers where a crafted path could escape the double quotes and execute arbitrary commands. The library did not provide a clear mechanism for users to specify the appropriate path transformation for their specific server environment, leading to an insecure default.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious remote path that, when processed by the server's scp command, would execute arbitrary commands on the server with the privileges of the scp process. This could lead to full system compromise.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/Renci.SshNet/ScpClient.cs
+++ b/src/Renci.SshNet/ScpClient.cs
@@ -155,9 +195,10 @@ private string EnsureIsDirectoryArg
         /// Initializes a new instance of the <see cref="ScpClient"/> class.
         /// </summary>
         /// <param name="connectionInfo">The connection info.</param>
+        /// <param name="remotePathTransformation">The transformation to apply to remote paths.</param>
         /// <exception cref="ArgumentNullException"><paramref name="connectionInfo"/> is <see langword="null"/>.</exception>
-        public ScpClient(ConnectionInfo connectionInfo)
-            : this(connectionInfo, ownsConnectionInfo: false)
+        public ScpClient(ConnectionInfo connectionInfo, IRemotePathTransformation remotePathTransformation)
+            : this(connectionInfo, ownsConnectionInfo: false, remotePathTransformation)
         {
         }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces new constructors for `ScpClient` that explicitly require an `IRemotePathTransformation` parameter, forcing users to consider and specify the correct path handling. It also marks the old constructors as obsolete and adds extensive documentation warning about the RCE risk and advising on choosing the appropriate transformation or using SFTP instead.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 95 occurrences**
> Languages: JavaScript, Python, Java, PHP, Go, Ruby, YAML, RUST, C#

## GHSA-465g-fh3v-9jw4 · github.com/rabbitmq/amqp091-go

2026-09-17 · Go · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→HEADER`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the URI string builder directly appended user-controlled values for TLS configuration parameters (CertFile, KeyFile, CACertFile, ServerName) into the query string without proper URL encoding. This allowed an attacker to inject arbitrary query parameters or overwrite existing ones by including '&' or '=' characters in the file paths or server name.

### Impact

An attacker could manipulate the connection's TLS configuration, potentially disabling TLS, forcing the use of specific certificates, or otherwise altering the security posture of the connection, leading to man-in-the-middle attacks or unauthorized access.

### Key diff

```
--- a/uri.go
+++ b/uri.go
@@ -229,27 +229,20 @@ func (uri URI) String() string {
 	}
 
 	if uri.CertFile != "" || uri.KeyFile != "" || uri.CACertFile != "" || uri.ServerName != "" {
-		rawQuery := strings.Builder{}
+		q := url.Values{}
 		if uri.CertFile != "" {
-			rawQuery.WriteString("certfile=")
-			rawQuery.WriteString(uri.CertFile)
-			rawQuery.WriteRune('&')
+			q.Set("certfile", uri.CertFile)
 		}
...
-		authority.RawQuery = rawQuery.String()
+		authority.RawQuery = q.Encode()
 	}
 
 	return authority.String()
```

### Fix summary

The patch replaces the manual string concatenation for query parameters with the `net/url` package's `url.Values` and `Encode()` method. This ensures that all user-provided values for TLS configuration parameters are correctly URL-encoded before being added to the URI's query string, preventing injection of arbitrary parameters.

## GHSA-4v58-74mf-rjx3 · github.com/rabbitmq/amqp091-go

2026-09-17 · Go · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the AMQP client did not validate the length of a field read from the network. A negative length value, when cast to an unsigned type for memory allocation, would result in a very large allocation, leading to a denial of service.

### Impact

An attacker could send a specially crafted AMQP message with a negative field length, causing the client to attempt to allocate an extremely large amount of memory, leading to a crash or resource exhaustion.

### Key diff

```
--- a/read.go
+++ b/read.go
@@ -255,6 +255,9 @@ func readField(r io.Reader) (v any, err error) {
 		if err = binary.Read(r, binary.BigEndian, &len); err != nil {
 			return nil, err
 		}
+		if len < 0 {
+			return nil, ErrSyntax
+		}
 
 		value := make([]byte, len)
 		if _, err = io.ReadFull(r, value); err != nil {
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds a check to ensure that the read length value is not negative. If a negative length is encountered, an ErrSyntax error is returned, preventing the large memory allocation.

## GHSA-c5pq-fr2g-9jpf · github.com/rabbitmq/amqp091-go

2026-09-17 · Go · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `INTEGER_OVERFLOW→BOUNDARY`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the `readLongstr` function did not properly handle an excessively large `length` value when reading a long string. While it checked if `length` exceeded `(^uint32(0) >> 1)` (max int32), it returned an empty string and no error, allowing the program to proceed with an invalid state.

### Impact

An attacker could send a crafted AMQP frame with an extremely large string length. This could lead to a protocol desynchronization, frame injection, or potentially other undefined behavior due to an integer overflow when allocating the buffer, possibly leading to denial of service or remote code execution.

### Key diff

```
--- a/read.go
+++ b/read.go
@@ -111,7 +111,7 @@ func readLongstr(r io.Reader) (v string, err error) {
 
 	// slices can't be longer than max int32 value
 	if length > (^uint32(0) >> 1) {
-		return
+		return "", ErrSyntax
 	}
 
 	bytes := make([]byte, length)
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the `readLongstr` function to return an `ErrSyntax` error immediately if the `length` of the string exceeds the maximum allowed `int32` value. This prevents the function from proceeding with an invalid length and ensures that malformed input is properly rejected.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 20 occurrences**
> Languages: Go, C#, RUST, PHP, Python, JavaScript
> → GHSA-r4fg-73rc-hhh7
> → GHSA-26qp-ffjh-2x4v
> → GHSA-v67w-737x-v2c9

> → GHSA-38c5-483c-4qqp

## GHSA-j497-x9hr-x34x · github.com/rabbitmq/amqp091-go

2026-09-17 · Go · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `INTEGER_OVERFLOW→BOUNDARY`

### Root cause

The `writeShortstr` function used a `uint8` to store the length of a string, which has a maximum value of 255. If a string longer than 255 bytes was provided, the length would silently wrap around (e.g., 256 becomes 0, 257 becomes 1), leading to data truncation and state corruption.

### Impact

An attacker could send a malformed AMQP frame with a short string exceeding 255 bytes. This would cause the string to be silently truncated on the receiving end, leading to data loss, protocol state corruption, and potentially unexpected application behavior or crashes.

### Key diff

```
--- a/write.go
+++ b/write.go
@@ -242,6 +242,9 @@ func writeFrame(w io.Writer, typ uint8, channel uint16, payload []byte) (err err
 
 func writeShortstr(w io.Writer, s string) (err error) {
 	b := []byte(s)
+	if len(b) > 255 {
+		return fmt.Errorf("amqp: shortstr %q exceeds 255 bytes", s)
+	}
 
 	length := uint8(len(b))
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds a check in the `writeShortstr` function to ensure that the length of the string does not exceed 255 bytes. If it does, an error is now returned, preventing the integer overflow and subsequent data truncation.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 20 occurrences**
> Languages: Go, C#, RUST, PHP, Python, JavaScript

## GHSA-xwwf-m8fg-p9q2 · github.com/rabbitmq/amqp091-go

2026-09-17 · Go · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the client library (amqp091-go) did not enforce the AMQP 0-9-1 specification's minimum frame size during negotiation. A malicious server could propose an extremely small frame size, which the client would accept, leading to excessive fragmentation and CPU overhead during message processing.

### Impact

An attacker controlling a malicious AMQP server could force a client connecting to it into a state of high CPU utilization, effectively causing a Denial of Service for the client application.

### Key diff

```
--- connection.go
+++ connection.go
@@ -1233,10 +1233,10 @@ func (c *Connection) openTune(config Config, auth Authentication) error {
 
 	c.m.Unlock()
 
-	// Frame size includes headers and end byte (len(payload)+8), even if
-	// this is less than FrameMinSize, use what the server sends because the
-	// alternative is to stop the handshake here.
-	c.Config.FrameSize = pick(config.FrameSize, int(tune.FrameMax))
+	// Frame size includes headers and end byte (len(payload)+8). Enforce the spec
+	// minimum floor of frameMinSize (4096 bytes) to prevent malicious servers
+	// from forcing extreme fragmentation and CPU overhead.
+	c.Config.FrameSize = negotiateFrameSize(config.FrameSize, int(tune.FrameMax))
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new function, `negotiateFrameSize`, which ensures that the negotiated frame size is at least `frameMinSize` (4096 bytes) as per the AMQP 0-9-1 specification. This prevents the client from accepting excessively small frame sizes proposed by a server.
